## Ecclesiastes - Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_d82s)

Introduction to Ecclesiastes
============================

Outline of Ecclesiastes
-----------------------

1. The author questions the nature of life and the limits of human wisdom (1:1–6:12\).
2. The author teaches about making wise choices in how one lives (7:1–12:7\)
3. The conclusion and ending (12:8–14\)

### What is the book of Ecclesiastes about?

Ecclesiastes is a group of short teachings that try to answer questions such as “What profit {belongs} to humanity in exchange for all his toil that he toils under the sun?” (1:3\) Ecclesiastes is a series of thoughts about a variety of subjects, all of them about the purpose and worth of various actions and events. The author concludes that all the work we perform and all the knowledge and skill we gain are as a vapor that disappears, and that we must fear Yahweh and keep his commandments.

### How should the title of this book be translated?

The traditional title for this book is “Ecclesiastes.” Its meaning is similar to “religious assembly.” Translators might use a different title such as “Words of a Teacher” or “Teachings of a Wise Man.”

### Who wrote the book of Ecclesiastes?

The writer calls himself Qoheleth and identifies himself as “the son of David, king in Jerusalem.” Jewish and Christian tradition identify Qoheleth with King Solomon based on:

* his wisdom (1:16 and twenty\-six other references to wisdom)
* his vast wealth (2:8\) and the pain that comes from wealth (4:8, 5:13–14, 9:11\)
* his large number of servants (2:7 and 10:7\)
* his limitless opportunities for worldly pleasures (2:1–2, 9, 3:13, 4:8, 5:4 and 12:1\) and
* the wide range of his many building projects (2:4–6\).

Some scholars think that Qoheleth was a later anonymous Jewish writer. He appears to have written the book of Ecclesiastes near the end of his life, as he reflected on what he gained from all that he did.

### Why are there so many apparent contradictions in the book of Ecclesiastes?

Some scholars think the author was a faithful man. Other scholars think that the author made bad decisions and was sorrowful when he wrote this book. Apparent contradictions in the book may indicate that Qoheleth’s faith was sometimes weak. Or it is also possible that the teachings in the book differ from each other in order to relate to different things happening in the lives of readers.

Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------

### What does this book teach about how God repays people?

In the ancient Near East, people were concerned with why they were being punished or blessed. They often connected these things to their gods. The writer of Ecclesiastes explains that Yahweh will bless and reward people for the right things they do and punish people for the evil things they do. But this might not happen in this life.

### What value did the Israelites place on the book of Ecclesiastes?

The Israelites have often questioned the value of this book. This is due in large part to its unusual wording and teachings. At times, it appears to disagree with the rest of Scripture. While many have questioned its authority, it has been affirmed to be Scripture. It gives valuable lessons concerning the uselessness of pursuing any goal other than to give Yahweh glory.

### Vapor as Futility

The Hebrew word translated as “vapor” recurs as a thematic refrain throughout Ecclesiastes, declaring human achievements fleeting. It indicates transience, not moral worthlessness. The paired phrase “pursuit of wind” intensifies this assessment.

Translation Issues
------------------

### What is the meaning of “under the sun?”

“Under the sun” here is another way of saying “on the earth.” When the author says that there is “nothing new under the sun,” this means that every kind of thing has already happened before on the earth. While a particular event may not have specifically occurred before, something similar has already occurred.
Translate this phrase consistently through the book.

### How do I translate harsh or shocking passages?

Parts of the book of Ecclesiastes can be shocking or surprising to read in Scripture. For example, “If a man should father a hundred {children}, and many years should he live—and many that the days of his years may be—but {if} his soul is not satisfied from the good {thing} (and also a burial place is not for him), I say, ‘Better than him {is} the stillborn child.’” (6:3\). The translator should allow these difficulties to remain and not try to make them less surprising.

### How is life described in the book of Ecclesiastes?

The author of Ecclesiastes concludes that everything in this life quickly passes away, like a vapor that disappears. A person’s circumstances or character and even all of the things of this world have little or no significance apart from God. That is because God gives meaning to everything.

At the end of their life, people often say that the years of their life seemed short. The author of Ecclesiastes used the metaphor of vapor or breath to describe how life ends while it seems to be just beginning.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 0:0


## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 1 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_zr9f)

Ecclesiastes 1 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

The chapter opens with a title (1\), a poem on the endless cycles of nature (2–11\), and Qoheleth’s first\-person search for wisdom (12–18\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Vapor

The Hebrew word rendered “vapor” is this book’s key term for what is fleeting and insubstantial. For a more thorough discussion of this, see the Introduction to Ecclesiastes.

### Under the sun

This recurring idiom means “in this life” or “on the earth.” See the Introduction to Ecclesiastes for a more thorough explanation of this phrase.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:0


## Ecclesiastes 1:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_tw68)

קֹהֶ֣לֶת

**"Qoheleth"**

The word **Qoheleth** is the name that the main speaker of this book chose to use for himself. In the original language it is a title that means “one who assembles a group,” that is, someone who gathers people together in order to teach them. You could spell out how this name sounds in your language, as the ULT does, or you could translate its meaning.

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:1
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); David (ID: `person:David`)

## Ecclesiastes 1:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_rpjq)

הֲבֵ֤ל הֲבָלִים֙

**"Vapor of vapors"**

Qoheleth is using **Vapor of vapors** as a common expression of his culture to mean “the most fleeting thing of all.” In this expression, a word is repeated in the possessive form in order to express a superlative. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The most fleeting thing of all] or \[Utterly temporary]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:2
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_k5cl)

הַ⁠כֹּ֥ל הָֽבֶל

**"the whole {is} vapor"**

Qoheleth says **the whole** as an extreme statement for emphasis. He does not mean that every single thing without exception is fleeting. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[just about everything is vapor]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:2
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_uugd)

מַה־יִּתְר֖וֹן לָֽ⁠אָדָ֑ם בְּ⁠כָל־עֲמָל֔⁠וֹ שֶֽׁ⁠יַּעֲמֹ֖ל תַּ֥חַת הַ⁠שָּֽׁמֶשׁ

**"What profit {belongs} to humanity in exchange for all his toil that he toils under the sun"**

Qoheleth is not using the question form to emphasize that people gain nothing lasting from all their labor. Instead, this is a genuine question. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[I wonder if there is any profit for a human in exchange for all his toil that he toils under the sun!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_bpe2)

יִּתְר֖וֹן

**"profit"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **profit**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[good thing that lasts]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_xbc4)

עֲמָל֔⁠וֹ שֶֽׁ⁠יַּעֲמֹ֖ל

**"his toil that he toils"**

Qoheleth is using a construction in which a verb and its object come from the same root. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this.

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_o2ag)

תַּ֥חַת הַ⁠שָּֽׁמֶשׁ

**"under the sun"**

Qoheleth is using **under the sun** as a common expression of his culture to mean “in this life on the earth.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. See the Introduction to Ecclesiastes for more information on translating this expression here and throughout this chapter. Alternate translation: \[in this earthly life]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ud0d)

דּ֤וֹר הֹלֵךְ֙ וְ⁠ד֣וֹר בָּ֔א

**"A generation is going and a generation is coming"**

Qoheleth is referring to the whole cycle of human life by naming its two major components, one generation **going** and another generation **coming**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[People are continually dying and being born]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_t6el)

שׁוֹאֵ֛ף זוֹרֵ֥חַֽ ה֖וּא שָֽׁם

**"panting, it is rising there"**

Qoheleth is speaking of the sun as if it were a living thing that could be **panting** as it hurried back to the place where it rises. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[it goes back quickly to rise there again]

See: Personification

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Personification (ID: `33302@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_jbxp)

אֶל־דָּר֔וֹם וְ⁠סוֹבֵ֖ב אֶל־צָפ֑וֹן

**"to {the} south and turning to {the} north"**

Qoheleth is referring to all directions by naming two major components, **south** and **north**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[in every direction]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:6
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_acz5)

הוֹלֵ֣ךְ הָ⁠ר֔וּחַ וְ⁠עַל־סְבִיבֹתָ֖י⁠ו שָׁ֥ב הָ⁠רֽוּחַ

**"the wind is going; and on its courses, the wind is returning"**

Qoheleth is speaking of **the wind** as if it were a living thing that could choose where to travel and then decide to return the same way. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the wind simply blows around and around in the same course]

See: Personification

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:6
* **Associated Articles:** Personification (ID: `33302@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_reh4)

וְ⁠לֹא־תִמָּלֵ֥א אֹ֖זֶן

**"and an ear is not filled"**

The expression **is not filled** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and an ear does not fill up]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_h3yv)

כָּל־הַ⁠דְּבָרִ֣ים יְגֵעִ֔ים

**"All the things {are} wearisome"**

Qoheleth says **All the things are wearisome** and **a person is not able to speak** as extreme statements for emphasis. He means that the endless repetition he has been describing is exhausting to consider and that no one could ever finish describing how wearisome these things are. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[Very many things indeed are wearisome; a person could never finish describing them]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_z31r)

אִ֖ישׁ

**"a person"**

Qoheleth is not referring to a specific **person**. He means people in general. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[anyone]

See: Generic Noun Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Generic Noun Phrases (ID: `33277@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_a2b0)

לֹא־תִשְׂבַּ֥ע עַ֨יִן֙ לִ⁠רְא֔וֹת וְ⁠לֹא־תִמָּלֵ֥א אֹ֖זֶן מִ⁠שְּׁמֹֽעַ

**"An eye is not satisfied of seeing, and an ear is not filled from hearing"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[People are never satisfied by anything they see or hear]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_fwia)

וּ⁠מַה־שֶׁ⁠נַּֽעֲשָׂ֔ה ה֖וּא שֶׁ⁠יֵּעָשֶׂ֑ה

**"and whatever that which has been done, it {is} that which will be done"**

The expressions **has been done** and **will be done** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the ideas in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and whatever people have done, it that which people will do]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:9
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_f6js)

תַּ֥חַת הַ⁠שָּֽׁמֶשׁ

**"under the sun"**

See how you translated the same expression in 1:3\.

## Ecclesiastes 1:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_fw9o)

יֵ֥שׁ דָּבָ֛ר שֶׁ⁠יֹּאמַ֥ר רְאֵה־זֶ֖ה חָדָ֣שׁ ה֑וּא

**"Is} there a thing about which one says, “See this, it {is} new"**

Qoheleth is using the question form to make an emphatic statement, that nothing anyone points to is actually new. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[There is nothing about which one says, “See this, it is new!”]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_nzok)

שֶׁ⁠יֹּאמַ֥ר

**"about which one says"**

The pronoun **one** refers to any person at all, not to a specific individual. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[about which anyone says]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_s4t0)

רְאֵה־זֶ֖ה חָדָ֣שׁ ה֑וּא

**"See this, it {is} new"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[that this thing is something new]

See: Quotes within Quotes

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:10
* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_frtj)

מִ⁠לְּ⁠פָנֵֽ⁠נוּ

**"from before our faces"**

Here, **our faces** represents “us,” that is, the people who are alive now. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[from before us]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

זִכְר֖וֹן

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **memory**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[anyone remembering]

## Ecclesiastes 1:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_wlwb)

לָ⁠רִאשֹׁנִ֑ים וְ⁠גַ֨ם לָ⁠אַחֲרֹנִ֜ים

**"the former {times}, and also, of the latter {times"**

Qoheleth is referring to people and events of every time by naming two major components, **the former times** and **the latter times**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the people and events of every time]

כָּל־אֲשֶׁ֥ר נַעֲשָׂ֖ה

The expression **is done** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[all that people are doing]

Qoheleth is using **I gave my heart** as a common expression of his culture to mean “I devoted my mind.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Ecclesiastes 1:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ilmh)

וּ⁠רְע֥וּת רֽוּחַ

**"and pursuit of wind"**

Qoheleth is speaking of everything people do as if it were a **pursuit of wind**. He means that gaining anything lasting from these deeds is as futile as trying to catch the wind. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and as futile as trying to catch the wind]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:14
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_c4zx)

וְ⁠הִנֵּ֥ה

**"and—behold"**

Qoheleth is using the term **behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation here and in verse 16\. Alternate translation: \[and—pay attention]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 1:14
* **Associated Articles:** Foreground Information (ID: `en_ta_translate_writing-foreground@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 1:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_i25v)

מְעֻוָּ֖ת לֹא־יוּכַ֣ל לִ⁠תְקֹ֑ן

**"A bent {thing}—one is not able to straighten {it"**

Qoheleth is speaking of what is wrong in the world as if it were a **bent** object that someone might try to make straight. He means that people cannot correct the things that are wrong with life. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[What is wrong in life—no one is able to make it right]

See: Metaphor

## Ecclesiastes 1:16 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_olgh)

וְ⁠לִבִּ֛⁠י רָאָ֥ה הַרְבֵּ֖ה חָכְמָ֥ה וָ⁠דָֽעַת

**"and my heart has seen much wisdom and knowledge"**

Qoheleth is speaking of his **heart** as if it were a person who could see. In this culture, the heart represented a person’s thoughts and understanding, and seeing something represented experiencing and knowing it. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and in my thinking I have experienced much wisdom and knowledge]

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 2 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_nbae)

Ecclesiastes 2 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Qoheleth narrates a first\-person experiment: he tests pleasure (1–11\), weighs wisdom against folly (12–23\), and concludes that enjoying food, drink, and work is God’s gift (24–26\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Vapor

See the Introduction to Ecclesiastes for a discussion of this term.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### The Heart

Qoheleth speaks with and to his “heart,” in Hebrew the seat of thinking and deciding rather than of emotion.

### Under the sun

See the Introduction to Ecclesiastes for a discussion of this phrase.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:0


## Ecclesiastes 2:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_e4br)

אָמַ֤רְתִּֽי אֲנִי֙ בְּ⁠לִבִּ֔⁠י

**"I spoke, I with my heart"**

Qoheleth is speaking of his own thoughts as if they were a person that he could talk to, his **heart**. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I thought to myself]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_cry4)

בְ⁠שִׂמְחָ֖ה וּ⁠רְאֵ֣ה בְ⁠ט֑וֹב

**"with joy and see good"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **joy**, you could express that idea in another way. Also, Qoheleth is speaking of **good** as if it were a thing that he could **see**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[by making you glad and letting you experience what is pleasant]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_qh3v)

וְ⁠הִנֵּ֥ה

**"But behold"**

Qoheleth is using the term **behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. See how you translated this word in 1:14\.

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Foreground Information (ID: `en_ta_translate_writing-foreground@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_cwxa)

גַם־ה֖וּא הָֽבֶל

**"it also {was} vapor"**

See how you translated this word in 1:14\.

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_fqvo)

לִ⁠שְׂח֖וֹק אָמַ֣רְתִּי מְהוֹלָ֑ל וּ⁠לְ⁠שִׂמְחָ֖ה

**"To laughter I said, “Madness!” And to joy"**

Qoheleth is speaking **To laughter** and **to joy** though he knows they cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening that he feels very strongly about these emotions and their place in life. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about them. Alternate translation: \[About laughter, I said, “Madness!” And about joy]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_yapi)

מְהוֹלָ֑ל

**"Madness"**

Qoheleth is using a word that expresses a strong feeling of scorn. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[Ridiculous]

See: Exclamations

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Exclamations (ID: `33270@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_k3g5)

וּ⁠לְ⁠שִׂמְחָ֖ה מַה־זֹּ֥ה עֹשָֽׂה

**"And to joy, “What is this doing"**

Qoheleth is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[And to joy, I said, “What is this doing]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_riym)

מַה־זֹּ֥ה עֹשָֽׂה

**"What is this doing"**

Qoheleth is using the question form to show that pleasure accomplishes nothing worthwhile. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[This accomplishes nothing!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:2
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 2:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_dece)

לִ⁠מְשׁ֥וֹךְ בַּ⁠יַּ֖יִן אֶת־בְּשָׂרִ֑⁠י

**"to drag my flesh with wine"**

Qoheleth is using **to drag my flesh with wine** as a common expression of the culture to mean “to gratify my body with wine.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[to gratify my body with wine]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:3
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`)

Qoheleth is speaking of his **heart** as if it were a living thing that could guide him. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and I was still thinking wisely all the while]

Here the masculine term **sons** has a generic sense that includes both men and women. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use language in your translation that is clearly inclusive of both men and women. See how you translated the same phrase in 1:13\.

תַּ֣חַת הַ⁠שָּׁמַ֔יִם

**"under the heavens"**

Qoheleth is using **under the heavens** as a common expression of the culture to mean “here on the earth during this life.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Ecclesiastes 2:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_av89)

בָּנִ֤יתִי לִ⁠י֙ בָּתִּ֔ים נָטַ֥עְתִּי לִ֖⁠י כְּרָמִֽים

**"I built for myself houses. I planted for myself vineyards"**

Qoheleth is using **I built for myself houses** and **I planted for myself vineyards** to represent the work that he had others do for him. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression from your culture or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I had houses built for myself. I had vineyards planted for myself]

If your readers would not be familiar with what royal parks are, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. **"and to {the} toil that I toiled to do"**

## Ecclesiastes 2:11 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_ekkb)

וְ⁠אֵ֥ין יִתְר֖וֹן תַּ֥חַת הַ⁠שָּֽׁמֶשׁ

**"and there was no profit under the sun"**

## Ecclesiastes 2:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_e5fy)

מֶ֣ה הָ⁠אָדָ֗ם שֶׁ⁠יָּבוֹא֙ אַחֲרֵ֣י הַ⁠מֶּ֔לֶךְ אֵ֥ת אֲשֶׁר־כְּבָ֖ר עָשֽׂוּ⁠הוּ

**"what {can} the person {do} who will come after the king {than} that which he has already done"**

Qoheleth is using the question form to emphasize that whoever comes after him will not be able to do anything more than what has already been done. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[the person who will come after the king can do nothing more than what has already been done.]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:12
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`)

## Ecclesiastes 2:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_u1c0)

מֶ֣ה הָ⁠אָדָ֗ם שֶׁ⁠יָּבוֹא֙ אַחֲרֵ֣י הַ⁠מֶּ֔לֶךְ

**"what {can} the person {do} who will come after the king"**

Qoheleth is not referring to a specific **person**. He means people in general who might come after him. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[what can any person do who will come after the king]

See: Generic Noun Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:12

## Ecclesiastes 2:12 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_ppgv)

אַחֲרֵ֣י הַ⁠מֶּ֔לֶךְ

**"after the king"**

Qoheleth is speaking of himself in the third person as **the king**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[after me]

## Ecclesiastes 2:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_nusw)

כִּֽ⁠יתְר֥וֹן הָ⁠א֖וֹר מִן־הַ⁠חֹֽשֶׁךְ

**"as {there is} a profit of the light more than the darkness"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the light** is more useful than **the darkness** for seeing where one is going, so wisdom is more useful than folly for living well. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as light helps a person to see where he is going, but darkness does not]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:13
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 2:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_pqu1)

הֶֽ⁠חָכָם֙ עֵינָ֣י⁠ו בְּ⁠רֹאשׁ֔⁠וֹ

**"The wise, his eyes {are} in his head"**

Qoheleth is using the expression that **his eyes are in his head** as a common expression of the culture to mean “he sees where he is going.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The wise, he sees where he is going]

## Ecclesiastes 2:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_yn39)

וְ⁠הַ⁠כְּסִ֖יל בַּ⁠חֹ֣שֶׁךְ הוֹלֵ֑ךְ

**"but the fool in the darkness is walking"**

Qoheleth is speaking of the way a foolish person lives as if it were **walking** in **the darkness**. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[but the fool lives without understanding]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 2:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_ugtf)

שֶׁ⁠מִּקְרֶ֥ה אֶחָ֖ד יִקְרֶ֥ה אֶת־כֻּלָּֽ⁠ם

**"that one happening will happen to both of them"**

Qoheleth is using a construction in which a verb and its subject come from the same root. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. The implication is that the one **happening** that will happen to both of them is death. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternatively, your language may have another way of expressing this. Alternate translation: \[that the same thing will occur to both of them] or \[that one thing, namely death, will happen to both of them]

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 2:14

## Ecclesiastes 2:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_fmqp)

**"As {the} happening of the fool, even {to} me—it will happen {to} me"**

Qoheleth is using a phrase that expresses a strong feeling of dismay. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[Alas, the very thing that happens to the fool will happen to me too]

**"And why was I, I, wise then, abundantly"**

Qoheleth is using the question form to express his frustration that becoming **wise** gained him no lasting advantage. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. ## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 3 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_awev)

Ecclesiastes 3 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter opens with a poem listing 14 pairs of contrasting activities assigned to appointed times (1–8\), then reflects on God’s sovereignty, human limitation, injustice, and the shared mortality of humans and animals (9–22\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Eternity in the human heart

In verse 11, God places eternity in the human heart yet limits understanding of his work, creating a tension central to the chapter’s argument.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 3:0

**"Also, he gives perpetuity in their heart, yet so that humanity cannot find the work that the God does from beginning to end"**

Here the word **yet** introduces the result of God giving the sense of duration **in their heart**: **humanity cannot find the work that the God does from beginning to end**. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that this is the purpose.

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 4 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_qb4y)

Ecclesiastes 4 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Qoheleth moves from observing oppression (1–3\) to reflecting on envy\-driven labor and proverbial sayings about contentment (4–6\), the advantages of companionship (7–12\), and a parable about a poor but wise youth replacing an old foolish king (13–16\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------

### Vapor as Futility

The Hebrew word translated as “vapor” recurs as a thematic refrain (vv. 4, 7–8, 16\), declaring human achievements fleeting. For a more complete discussion, see the Introduction to Ecclesiastes.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Ambiguous Pronoun References

In the parable of the youth and the king (13–16\), Hebrew pronouns shift referents without explicit markers. Translators may need to clarify whether “he” refers to the youth or the king.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:0


## Ecclesiastes 4:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_xno3)

וְ⁠שַׁ֣בְתִּֽי אֲנִ֗י

**"And I turned, I"**

The pronoun **I** refers to Qoheleth. Here, and other places throughout this chapter, it is stated separately from the verb for emphasis. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And I, Qoheleth, turned]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_i4eo)

אֶת־כָּל־הָ֣⁠עֲשֻׁקִ֔ים

**"all the oppressions"**

Qoheleth says **all the oppressions** as an extreme statement for emphasis. He means very many oppressions, not necessarily every single one. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[very many oppressions]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_vr69)

אֶת־כָּל־עָמָ֗ל וְ⁠אֵת֙ כָּל־כִּשְׁר֣וֹן הַֽ⁠מַּעֲשֶׂ֔ה

**"every toil and every success of the deed"**

The terms **every toil** and **every success of the deed** mean similar things. Qoheleth is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[all that they accomplished]

## Ecclesiastes 4:4 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_wedv)

קִנְאַת־אִ֖ישׁ מֵ⁠רֵעֵ֑⁠הוּ

**"is} the envy of a person from his friend"**

Qoheleth is using the possessive form to describe a **deed** that is characterized by **envy of a person from his friend**, meaning that people’s work is driven by jealousy of their neighbors. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[is motivated by a person’s envy of his neighbor]

## Ecclesiastes 4:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_mlvf)

הַ⁠כְּסִיל֙ חֹבֵ֣ק אֶת־יָדָ֔י⁠ו וְ⁠אֹכֵ֖ל אֶת־בְּשָׂרֽ⁠וֹ

**"The fool is folding his hands and is eating his flesh"**

Qoheleth uses a proverb in order to illustrate the self\-destructive nature of laziness. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. Alternate translation: \[The fool folds his hands and eats his own flesh]

See: Proverbs

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Proverbs (ID: `33425@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_k3tz)

חֹבֵ֣ק אֶת־יָדָ֔י⁠ו

**"is folding his hands"**

Qoheleth is using **is folding his hands** as a common expression of the culture to mean “is being lazy” or “is refusing to work.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[is being lazy]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_lwl1)

וְ⁠אֹכֵ֖ל אֶת־בְּשָׂרֽ⁠וֹ

**"and is eating his flesh"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if the fool’s laziness were causing him to consume his own **flesh**. He means that the fool’s idleness destroys himself. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and is destroying himself]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_j8c7)

ט֕וֹב מְלֹ֥א כַ֖ף נָ֑חַת מִ⁠מְּלֹ֥א חָפְנַ֛יִם עָמָ֖ל וּ⁠רְע֥וּת רֽוּחַ

**"Better {is} a fullness of a palm {with} quiet than a fullness of {two} handfuls {with} toil and pursuit of wind"**

Qoheleth uses a proverb in order to teach that contentment with little is better than striving for much. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. Alternate translation: \[One handful with peace is better than two handfuls with toil and chasing after wind]

See: Proverbs

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Proverbs (ID: `33425@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 4:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_gi43)

מְלֹ֥א כַ֖ף נָ֑חַת

**"is} a fullness of a palm {with} quiet"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if having a small amount with peace were having **a fullness of a palm with quiet**. He means that having few possessions and being content is valuable. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[a small amount with peace]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:8 (#5) (id: en_tn_ECC_s7u4)

וּ⁠לְ⁠מִ֣י ׀ אֲנִ֣י עָמֵ֗ל וּ⁠מְחַסֵּ֤ר אֶת־נַפְשִׁ⁠י֙ מִ⁠טּוֹבָ֔ה

**"So why {am} I toiling and causing my soul to lack from good things"**

## Ecclesiastes 4:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_whwh)

כִּ֣י אִם־יִפֹּ֔לוּ

**"For if they fall"**

## Ecclesiastes 4:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_y234)

וְ⁠אִ֣י⁠ל֗⁠וֹ

**"but woe {is} his"**

Qoheleth is using a phrase that expresses a strong feeling of distress for the person who is alone and falls with no one to help. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[but how terrible it will be for him]

See: Exclamations

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:10
* **Associated Articles:** Exclamations (ID: `33270@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 4:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ielm)

וְ⁠אִֽם־יִתְקְפ⁠וֹ֙ הָ⁠אֶחָ֔ד

**"And if he will overpower him, the one {person"**

The pronoun **he** refers to an attacker, while the pronoun **him** refers to the person who is alone. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And if an attacker overpowers the one person]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:12
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 4:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_m8k7)

וְ⁠הַ⁠חוּט֙ הַֽ⁠מְשֻׁלָּ֔שׁ לֹ֥א בִ⁠מְהֵרָ֖ה יִנָּתֵֽק

**"and the three\-fold rope will not be torn in two quickly"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if the bond between three people were a **three\-fold rope** that cannot easily be **torn in two**. He means that three people united together are very strong. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and three people standing together will not be quickly defeated]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 4:12
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Thread (ID: `realia:Thread`)

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 5 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_nipz)

Ecclesiastes 5 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

The chapter moves from instruction about worship and vows (1–7\) to reflection on oppression and wealth (8–17\), closing with counsel to enjoy God’s gifts (18–20\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Vows

A person could promise God to do something in return for help. Such a promise was binding, so the writer warns against vowing rashly.

### Vapor

The writer calls things “vapor” (7, 10\), a recurring image for what is fleeting and empty. See the Introduction to Ecclesiastes for a further explanation of this term and its usage.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 5:0


## Ecclesiastes 5:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_o2sh)

שְׁמֹ֣ר רגלי⁠ך

**"Keep your feet"**

Qoheleth is using **Keep your feet** as a common expression of the culture to mean “be careful about how you conduct yourself.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Watch your conduct]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 5:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Ecclesiastes 5:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_jeuz)

כִּ֛י בָּ֥א הַ⁠חֲל֖וֹם בְּ⁠רֹ֣ב עִנְיָ֑ן וְ⁠ק֥וֹל כְּסִ֖יל בְּ⁠רֹ֥ב דְּבָרִֽים

**"For the dream comes with much business, and the voice of a fool with many words"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[For a fool is certainly known by his excessive talking]

## Ecclesiastes 5:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_ol0d)

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 6 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_rofb)

Ecclesiastes 6 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Qoheleth continues his examination of wealth and mortality, arguing that neither abundance nor long life benefits a person who is unable to enjoy them.

1. Wealth that God gives but does not allow a person to enjoy (1–2\)
2. A long, unsatisfied life is worse than a stillborn child (3–6\)
3. Sayings about appetite, wisdom, and desire (7–9\)
4. Human limits before the one who is more powerful (10–12\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 6:7 and 6:9\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Vapor

The word rendered “vapor” recurs through this chapter (verses 2, 4, 9, 11, and 12\) as the book’s key image for what is fleeting and insubstantial, not for what is morally worthless.

### Burial

In this culture a proper burial honored the dead, so the remark that “a burial place is not for him” (3\) describes a disgrace rather than merely an absence.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Rhetorical Questions

Qoheleth advances much of his argument by asking questions that expect no answer (verses 6, 8, 11, and 12\). If questions do not work this way in your language, you could translate them as statements.

### Quoted Sayings

Verses 7 and 9 appear to be popular sayings that Qoheleth quotes in support of his argument. If it would be helpful, you could mark them as quoted sayings.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 6:0

## Ecclesiastes 6:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_jgkr)

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 7 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_nz0g)

Ecclesiastes 7 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter first gathers proverbs about wisdom, mortality, and the limits of human understanding, and then records Qoheleth’s own search for understanding.

1. Better\-than proverbs about mourning, grief, and rebuke (1–6\)
2. Further proverbs about patience, anger, and the protection wisdom gives (7–12\)
3. A call to consider God’s work in good times and bad (13–14\)
4. Counsel about righteousness, wisdom, and human sinfulness (15–22\)
5. Qoheleth’s search for the scheme of things (23–29\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 7:1–12, 14–18, and 26–29\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Better\-than sayings

Many verses in this chapter use a proverb form that sets two things side by side and declares one of them more advantageous than the other. The comparison is practical rather than absolute: it commends what teaches a person about life, not what feels pleasant.

### Vapor

The Hebrew word rendered “vapor” is this book’s key term for what is fleeting and insubstantial, and it appears again in this chapter in verses 6 and 15\. Render it consistently through the book.

### Fearing God

In verse 18, the person who fears God is the person who reverently acknowledges God’s rule and lives accordingly. This is respectful submission, not terror.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### The heart

Throughout the chapter, “heart” stands for the inner person: thoughts, inclination, and moral judgment. If your readers would not understand this use of “heart,” consider a term in your language that refers to the seat of thinking and deciding.

### The woman in verse 26

Interpreters understand the woman in verse 26 in two ways: (1\) a specific kind of seductive woman who entraps men or (2\) folly pictured as a woman. The ULT preserves the figure so that either reading remains possible.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:0


## Ecclesiastes 7:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_gbey)

ט֥וֹב שֵׁ֖ם מִ⁠שֶּׁ֣מֶן ט֑וֹב

**"Better is a {good} name than good oil"**

Here Qoheleth uses or invents a proverb in order to teach that what people remember about a person is worth more than what money can buy. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. Alternate translation: \[A good name is better than fine perfume]

See: Proverbs

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:1

## Ecclesiastes 7:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_g40s)

כְ⁠ק֤וֹל הַ⁠סִּירִים֙ תַּ֣חַת הַ⁠סִּ֔יר כֵּ֖ן שְׂחֹ֣ק הַ⁠כְּסִ֑יל

**"like the sound of the thorns under the cooking pot, so {is} the laughter of the fool"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the sound of the thorns under the cooking pot** is loud but produces almost no heat, so is **the laughter of the fool** loud but worth nothing. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[just as thorns crackle loudly under the cooking pot but give almost no heat, so the laughter of the fool is loud but accomplishes nothing]

See: Simile

## Ecclesiastes 7:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_iz69)

כִּ֥י הָ⁠עֹ֖שֶׁק יְהוֹלֵ֣ל חָכָ֑ם וִֽ⁠יאַבֵּ֥ד אֶת־לֵ֖ב מַתָּנָֽה

**"Surely oppression crazes {a} wise {person}, and a bribe destroys a heart"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[Surely corrupt gain completely ruins the mind of even a wise person]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:7
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_ka88)

הֱיֵ֣ה בְ⁠ט֔וֹב

**"be in good"**

Qoheleth is using **be in good** as a common expression of the culture to mean “enjoy what is good.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[enjoy the good things]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_swhq)

אֶת־זֶ֤ה לְ⁠עֻמַּת־זֶה֙ עָשָׂ֣ה הָֽ⁠אֱלֹהִ֔ים

**"the God has done this as well as this"**

The first occurrence of the pronoun **this** refers to **a day of good**, while the second refers to **a day of misfortune**. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[the God has made the day of good as well as the day of misfortune]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:14 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_mr5p)

שֶׁ⁠לֹּ֨א יִמְצָ֧א הָֽ⁠אָדָ֛ם אַחֲרָ֖י⁠ו מְאֽוּמָה

**"humanity cannot find anything after him"**

Qoheleth is using **cannot find anything after him** as a common expression of the culture to mean “cannot discover what God has planned.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[humanity cannot discover what God has planned]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_p3f3)

בִּ⁠ימֵ֣י הֶבְלִ֑⁠י

**"in the days of my vapor"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if his lifetime were **vapor**, which appears for a moment and then is gone. See how you translated the similar expression in 1:14\. Alternate translation: \[in the days of my brief life]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 7:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_hx8g)

אֹבֵ֣ד בְּ⁠צִדְק֔⁠וֹ

**"perishing in his righteousness"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **his righteousness**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[perishing even though he acts righteously]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 7:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_c44n)

מַאֲרִ֖יךְ בְּ⁠רָעָתֽ⁠וֹ

**"lengthening in his evil"**

Qoheleth is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[lengthening his days in his evil]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 7:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_abem)

אַל־תְּהִ֤י צַדִּיק֙ הַרְבֵּ֔ה

**"Do not be overly righteous"**

The implication is that Qoheleth is warning against a kind of excess, not against being righteous. This could mean: (1\) being self\-righteous. Alternate translation: \[Do not be self\-righteous]; or (2\) being excessively scrupulous about doing what is right. Alternate translation: \[Do not be excessively scrupulous]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:16
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 7:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_nz2v)

לָ֖⁠מָּה תִּשּׁוֹמֵֽם

**"why should you be shocked"**

The expression **be shocked** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[why should life shock you]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:16
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 7:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ybkp)

לָ֥⁠מָּה תָמ֖וּת בְּ⁠לֹ֥א עִתֶּֽ⁠ךָ

**"why should you die when {it is} not your time"**

Qoheleth is using the question form to warn his readers about the consequence of being wicked and foolish. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[otherwise you will die before your time has come!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Act Wickedly (ID: `keyterm:ActWickedly`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_bcrn)

בְּ⁠לֹ֥א עִתֶּֽ⁠ךָ

**"when {it is} not your time"**

Qoheleth is using **not your time** as a common expression of the culture to mean “before the time when you would otherwise die.” This could mean: (1\) that the person dies needlessly young because of their own foolishness. Alternate translation: \[sooner than you need to]; or (2\) that God cuts their life short. Alternate translation: \[because God is displeased with your wickedness]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Act Wickedly (ID: `keyterm:ActWickedly`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ueb7)

תֶּאֱחֹ֣ז בָּ⁠זֶ֔ה וְ⁠גַם־מִ⁠זֶּ֖ה

**"you should take hold of this, and also from this"**

The first occurrence of the pronoun **this** refers to the warning against being overly righteous and wise, while the second refers to the warning against being wicked and foolish. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[you should take hold of this warning against being overly righteous and wise, and also from that warning against being wicked and foolish]

**"you should take hold of this"**

Qoheleth is using **do not let your hand rest** as a common expression of the culture to mean “do not let go of it.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. **"will go forth with both of them"**

Qoheleth is using **will go forth with both of them** as a common expression of the culture to mean “will come safely through both of these.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Ecclesiastes 7:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_kj05)

הַֽ⁠חָכְמָ֖ה תָּעֹ֣ז לֶ⁠חָכָ֑ם מֵֽ⁠עֲשָׂרָה֙ שַׁלִּיטִ֔ים אֲשֶׁ֥ר הָי֖וּ בָּ⁠עִֽיר

**"Wisdom is strong for the wise, more than ten rulers who are in the city"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if wisdom were a defense that makes a person **strong**, the way that **rulers** defend a city. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Wisdom protects the wise person better than ten rulers can protect the city]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 7:19
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_ygfe)

לֶ⁠חָכָ֑ם

**"for the wise"**

Qoheleth is using the adjective **the wise** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[for a wise person]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:19 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_z9wx)

מֵֽ⁠עֲשָׂרָה֙ שַׁלִּיטִ֔ים אֲשֶׁ֥ר הָי֖וּ בָּ⁠עִֽיר

**"more than ten rulers who are in the city"**

Qoheleth says **ten rulers** as an extreme statement for emphasis. He means a large number of powerful officials. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[more than a whole group of powerful rulers in the city]

## Ecclesiastes 7:20 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_y8rv)

**"For a person—there is not a righteous {person} on the earth"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the information about **a person** with the information about there not being **a righteous {person} on the earth**. Alternate translation: \[For there is not a righteous person among the people on the earth]

* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 7:20 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_haqu)

אֵ֥ין צַדִּ֖יק בָּ⁠אָ֑רֶץ אֲשֶׁ֥ר יַעֲשֶׂה־טּ֖וֹב וְ⁠לֹ֥א יֶחֱטָֽא

**"there is not a righteous {person} on the earth who does good and does not sin"**

Here Qoheleth uses or invents a proverb in order to state that every person sins. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. Alternate translation: \[no righteous man walks the earth who does only good and never sins]

**"to all of the words that they say, do not give your heart"**

**"your servant belittling you"**

The implication is that it would be especially painful to hear **your servant** speak badly of you, since a servant owed his master respect. **"your heart knows"**

Qoheleth is using **your heart knows** as a common expression of the culture to mean “you yourself are aware.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[you know]

## Ecclesiastes 7:22 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_slun)

גַּם־את קִלַּ֥לְתָּ אֲחֵרִֽים

**"even you have belittled others"**

Qoheleth is stating the pronoun **you** separately, even though the verb translated as **have belittled** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that his readers have done the very thing that they would not want to hear. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[even you yourself have belittled others]

**"I turned, I, and my heart"**

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 8 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_tcbh)

Ecclesiastes 8 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter moves from wise conduct before a king to the injustice that Qoheleth observes, and it ends by affirming that no one can fully understand what God does.

1. The value of wisdom (1\)
2. Wise conduct before a king (2–9\)

    1. Obeying the king’s command (2–4\)
        2. Knowing the right time and the limits of human power (5–9\)
3. The wicked seem to escape judgment (10–14\)
4. Commendation of joy (15\)
5. The limits of understanding God’s work (16–17\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 8:1, 8:5–8, and 8:11–14\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The oath of God

In verse 2, Qoheleth grounds obedience to the king in an oath connected with God. This likely refers to a promise of loyalty to the king that people made in God’s name, though it could refer to God’s own oath concerning the king.

### Vapor

The Hebrew word rendered “vapor” is this book’s key term for what is fleeting and insubstantial, and it appears again in this chapter in verses 10 and 14\. Render it consistently through the book.

### Fearing God

In verses 12 and 13, those who fear God are people who reverently acknowledge his rule and live accordingly. This is respectful submission, not terror.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Generic “him”

In several verses (6, 7, 15, and 17\), “him” and “he” refer to people in general rather than to a specific person. If your language would not use a singular pronoun this way, you could use a plural or general expression.

### Textual variant in verse 10

Most Hebrew manuscripts say that the wicked were “forgotten” in the city, while some manuscripts and ancient versions say that they were “praised.” The ULT follows the second reading.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 8:0


## Ecclesiastes 8:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_xg0x)

מִ֚י כְּ⁠הֶ֣⁠חָכָ֔ם וּ⁠מִ֥י יוֹדֵ֖עַ פֵּ֣שֶׁר דָּבָ֑ר

**"Who {is} like the wise person, and who knows {the} interpretation of a matter"**

Qoheleth is using the question form to emphasize that no one is like the wise person, because only a wise person knows the interpretation of a matter. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate these questions as statements or as exclamations. Alternate translation: \[No one is like the wise person, and no one else knows the interpretation of a matter!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 8:1

## Ecclesiastes 8:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_fqne)

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 9 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_jwvc)

Ecclesiastes 9 Chapter Introduction
===================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter reflects on death as the one event that comes to every person, urges readers to enjoy life while they can, and ends with a story showing that wisdom is valuable even when people do not honor it.

1. The same fate comes to everyone (1–3\)
2. The living have hope, but the dead are forgotten (4–6\)
3. A call to enjoy life and work with all your strength (7–10\)
4. Time and misfortune overtake all people (11–12\)
5. The value of wisdom (13–18\)

    1. The poor wise man who delivered a city (13–15\)
        2. Sayings about wisdom (16–18\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 9:2, 9:4–6, 9:11–12, and 9:17–18\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Sheol

In verse 10, Hades is the place of the dead, where Qoheleth says there is no more work, planning, knowledge, or wisdom. Use a term that refers to the place where people go when they die, not a place of punishment.

### White clothes and oil

In verse 8, wearing white clothes and putting oil on the head were signs of joy and celebration in the culture of that time. If your readers would not understand these as signs of joy, you could explain them in a footnote.

### Vapor

The Hebrew word rendered “vapor” is this book’s key term for what is fleeting and insubstantial, and it appears twice in verse 9\. Render it consistently through the book.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### “One happening”

In verses 2 and 3, the “one happening” that comes to everyone is death. Translate this in a way that makes clear that Qoheleth is speaking about the fate that all people share, whether they are righteous or wicked.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:0

Qoheleth is using **I gave to my heart** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I thought carefully about.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I carefully considered]

**"one happening"**

The implication is that the **one happening** that comes to everyone is death. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the one event of death]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_ohzo)

לַ⁠צַּדִּ֤יק וְ⁠לָ⁠רָשָׁע֙ לַ⁠טּוֹב֙ וְ⁠לַ⁠טָּה֣וֹר וְ⁠לַ⁠טָּמֵ֔א וְ⁠לַ⁠זֹּבֵ֔חַ וְ⁠לַ⁠אֲשֶׁ֖ר אֵינֶ֣⁠נּוּ זֹבֵ֑חַ

**"for the righteous person and for the wicked person, for the good person and for the pure person and for the unclean person, and for the person who sacrifices and for the person who does not sacrifice"**

Qoheleth is referring to all kinds of people by naming pairs of major components that are opposites, such as **the righteous person** and **the wicked person**, **the pure person** and **the unclean person**, and **the person who sacrifices** and **the person who does not sacrifice**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[for every kind of person, whether they are righteous or wicked, good and pure or unclean, whether they sacrifice or do not sacrifice]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:2
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_c7se)

לַ⁠טּוֹב֙ וְ⁠לַ⁠טָּה֣וֹר

**"for the good person and for the pure person"**

The standard written Hebrew text as we have it today reads “for the good person and for the pure person.” The ULT follows that reading. Other ancient manuscripts read “for the good person and for the evil person and for the pure person.” If a translation of the Bible exists in your region, you may wish to use the reading that it uses. If a translation of the Bible does not exist in your region, you may wish to use the reading of the ULT.

See: Textual Variants

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:2
* **Associated Articles:** Textual Variants (ID: `33405@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:2 (#5) (id: en_tn_ECC_igpj)

כַּ⁠טּוֹב֙ כַּֽ⁠חֹטֶ֔א הַ⁠נִּשְׁבָּ֕ע

**"As the good person, so the sinner, the person who swears {an oath"**

Qoheleth is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[As it is for the good person, so it is for the sinner; in the same way, as it is for the person who swears an oath, he]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:2
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:2 (#6) (id: en_tn_ECC_bnbu)

הַ⁠נִּשְׁבָּ֕ע כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁ֖ר שְׁבוּעָ֥ה יָרֵֽא

**"the \& person who swears {an oath is} as the person who fears an oath"**

The implication is that **the person who swears** an oath is someone who makes oaths carelessly, while **the person who fears an oath** is someone who respects oaths so much that he is careful about making them. Qoheleth means that the same thing happens to both kinds of people. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the person who carelessly swears an oath is as the person who is careful about making oaths]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:2
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_cvlu)

לֵ֣ב בְּֽנֵי־הָ֠⁠אָדָם מָלֵא־רָ֨ע וְ⁠הוֹלֵל֤וֹת בִּ⁠לְבָבָ⁠ם֙

**"the heart of the sons of humanity {is} full of evil, and madness {is} in their heart"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if **the heart** of people were a container that is **full of evil** and that has **madness** in it. He means that people constantly think about and desire evil and foolish things. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the sons of humanity constantly think about and desire evil things, and they think about foolish things]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ga9t)

מִי֙ אֲשֶׁ֣ר יבחר אֶ֥ל כָּל־הַ⁠חַיִּ֖ים

**"whoever that is joined to all the living"**

Qoheleth is using **whoever that is joined to all the living** as a common expression of the culture to mean “whoever is still alive.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Ecclesiastes 9:4 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_qgfa)

יבחר

**"is joined"**

The phrase **is joined** is a translation of the correction that later Hebrew scribes recommended in the margin of the text. The written Hebrew text has a word with the same letters in a different order, which means “is chosen.” The ULT follows the correction in the margin, and you may want to do the same or follow the reading of the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[is chosen to belong]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 9:4
* **Associated Articles:** Alternative Readings (ID: `en_ta_translate_translate-alternativereadings@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Dog (ID: `fauna:Dog`)

## Ecclesiastes 9:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_o0e0)

כִּֽי־לְ⁠כֶ֤לֶב חַי֙ ה֣וּא ט֔וֹב מִן־הָ⁠אַרְיֵ֖ה הַ⁠מֵּֽת

**"for a living dog— it {is} better than the dead lion"**

The implication is that people in this culture despised dogs but considered the **lion** to be a noble and powerful animal. Qoheleth means that even the lowliest person who is alive is better off than the greatest person who has died. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[for even a living dog, which people despise, is better off than the dead lion, which people honor]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

Qoheleth is speaking as if the **love**, **hate**, and **jealousy** of dead people were living things that have **perished**. He means that these feelings have ended. ## Ecclesiastes 9:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_a1a5)

וְ⁠חֵ֨לֶק אֵין־לָ⁠הֶ֥ם

**"And there is not for them a portion"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if the good things that people can experience in life were a **portion**, that is, a share of property that someone receives. He means that dead people can no longer take part in or enjoy anything that happens in this life. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And there is nothing for them to enjoy]

## Ecclesiastes 9:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_xd0n)

לֵ֣ךְ אֱכֹ֤ל בְּ⁠שִׂמְחָה֙ לַחְמֶ֔⁠ךָ וּֽ⁠שֲׁתֵ֥ה בְ⁠לֶב־ט֖וֹב יֵינֶ֑⁠ךָ

**"Go! Eat your bread with joy, and drink your wine with a good heart"**

These are imperatives, but they communicate encouragement rather than commands. If the imperatives do not communicate encouragement for your readers, then use a form in your language that communicates encouragement. ## Ecclesiastes 9:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_r9cn)

יִהְי֥וּ בְגָדֶ֖י⁠ךָ לְבָנִ֑ים וְ⁠שֶׁ֖מֶן עַל־רֹאשְׁ⁠ךָ֥ אַל־יֶחְסָֽר

**"let your clothes be white, and do not let oil be lacking on your head"**

Wearing white clothes and putting oil on the head were symbolic actions by which people showed that they were joyful and celebrating. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of these actions in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[let your clothes be white to show that you are joyful, and do not let oil be lacking on your head as a sign that you are celebrating]
