## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 10 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_w3dy)

Ecclesiastes 10 Chapter Introduction
====================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is a collection of short wisdom sayings that contrast the wise person with the foolish person, especially in how each one acts, works, and speaks, and in how rulers govern.

1. A little folly ruins much wisdom (1–3\)
2. Rulers and those under them (4–7\)

    1. Stay calm when a ruler is angry (4\)
        2. Rulers who give high positions to the wrong people (5–7\)
3. Wisdom and the dangers of ordinary work (8–11\)
4. The words of the wise and the words of fools (12–15\)
5. Sayings about rulers, work, and speech (16–20\)

    1. Foolish rulers and noble rulers (16–17\)
        2. Laziness, feasting, and money (18–19\)
        3. Do not curse the king even in private (20\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 10:1–20\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Right and left

In verse 2, the right side was associated with strength, protection, and favor and the left side with weakness and misfortune. If your readers would not understand this association, you could express the meaning plainly or explain it in a footnote.

### Snake charming

In verse 11, the “master of the tongue” is a snake charmer, a person who used words or sounds to calm snakes so they would not bite. His skill is useless if the snake bites before he uses it.

### Feasting in the morning

In verse 16, princes who “eat in the morning” are feasting when they should be working, which showed that they did not take their responsibilities seriously.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Proverbs

Most verses in this chapter are proverbs that are only loosely connected to one another. Translate them in a way that sounds like a wise saying in your language and keep each one short and memorable.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:0


## Ecclesiastes 10:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_dbum)

זְב֣וּבֵי מָ֔וֶת

**"Flies of death"**

Qoheleth is using the possessive form to describe **Flies** that are characterized by **death**. This could mean: (1\) flies that have died. Alternate translation: \[Dead flies]; or (2\) flies that bring death or disease. Alternate translation: \[Flies that bring death] If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way.

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:1
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Fly (ID: `fauna:Fly`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_m2kf)

יָקָ֛ר מֵ⁠חָכְמָ֥ה מִ⁠כָּב֖וֹד סִכְל֥וּת מְעָֽט

**"weightier than wisdom or honor {is} a little foolishness"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **Flies of death** can spoil a whole container of **a perfumer’s oil**, so **a little foolishness** can spoil the good that **wisdom** and **honor** have accomplished. Qoheleth implies this comparison by placing the two statements next to each other. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[in the same way, a little foolishness can spoil the good that wisdom or honor has accomplished]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:1
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Fly (ID: `fauna:Fly`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_zrk5)

לֵ֤ב \& וְ⁠לֵ֥ב

**"The} heart of \& but a heart of"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if a person’s mind and will were his **heart**. It means that what a person thinks and desires guides the way he lives. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The mind of … but the mind of]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_st3o)

חָכָם֙ \& כְּסִ֖יל

**"a wise person \& a fool"**

Qoheleth is not referring to a specific **wise person** or a specific **fool**. He means wise people and foolish people in general. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[whoever is wise … whoever is foolish]

See: Generic Noun Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:2
* **Associated Articles:** Generic Noun Phrases (ID: `33277@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_ob6v)

לִֽ⁠ימִינ֔⁠וֹ \& לִ⁠שְׂמֹאלֽ⁠וֹ

**"to his right \& to his left"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if the heart could turn **to his right** or **to his left**. In this culture, the right side represented strength and favor, and the left side represented weakness or misfortune. This could mean: (1\) a wise person’s understanding protects him, while a fool is exposed to harm. Alternate translation: \[it can protect him … it leaves him defenseless]; or (2\) a wise person is inclined toward what is good, while a fool is inclined toward what is evil. Alternate translation: \[it leads him toward what is good … it leads him toward what is evil]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_gluz)

לִבּ֣⁠וֹ חָסֵ֑ר

**"his heart {is} lacking"**

Qoheleth is using the expression that **his heart is lacking** as a common expression of the culture to mean “he lacks good sense.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[his mind lacks good sense]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:3
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_rslt)

וְ⁠אָמַ֥ר לַ⁠כֹּ֖ל

**"and it says to everyone {that} he"**

Qoheleth is speaking of the fool’s lack of sense as if it were a living thing that could speak to **everyone**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and his behavior shows everyone that he]

See: Personification

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:3
* **Associated Articles:** Personification (ID: `33302@UWTranslationManual`)

ר֤וּחַ הַ⁠מּוֹשֵׁל֙ תַּעֲלֶ֣ה עָלֶ֔י⁠ךָ

**"the spirit of the ruler rises against you"**

Qoheleth is using **the spirit of the ruler rises against you** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the ruler becomes angry with you.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. מְקוֹמְ⁠ךָ֖ אַל־תַּנַּ֑ח

**"do not leave your place"**

Qoheleth is using **do not leave your place** as a common expression of the culture to mean “do not abandon your position.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[do not resign from your post]

מַרְפֵּ֔א

**"calmness"**

## Ecclesiastes 10:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_xuo1)

יַנִּ֖יחַ חֲטָאִ֥ים גְּדוֹלִֽים

**"will cause great sins to leave"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if **great sins** were things that could be caused **to leave**. This could mean: (1\) staying calm will cause the ruler to overlook great offenses. Alternate translation: \[can reduce the king’s anger at the offenses]; or (2\) staying calm will keep you from committing great sins, such as rebelling against the ruler. Alternate translation: \[can keep great offenses from having their effect]

תַּ֣חַת הַ⁠שָּׁ֑מֶשׁ

See how you translated **under the sun** in 1:3\.

## Ecclesiastes 10:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_pw06)

כִּ⁠שְׁגָגָ֕ה

**"like an unintentional sin"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **an unintentional sin** that a ruler commits causes harm even though he did not intend it, so is the **misfortune** that Qoheleth saw harmful. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[which was harmful like an unintentional sin]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:5
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_fyso)

מִ⁠לִּ⁠פְנֵ֥י הַ⁠שַּׁלִּֽיט

**"from before the face of the authority"**

Here, **the face** represents the presence of **the authority**. The expression means that the error comes from the ruler himself. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[from the authority himself]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_eg09)

נִתַּ֣ן הַ⁠סֶּ֔כֶל

**"Folly is placed"**

The expression **Folly is placed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. If you must state who did the action, it is the ruler mentioned in the previous verse. Alternate translation: \[The ruler places folly] or \[The ruler places foolish people]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:6
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_fjen)

הַ⁠סֶּ֔כֶל

**"Folly"**

Here, **Folly** represents foolish people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[The foolish person]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_z9jw)

בַּ⁠מְּרוֹמִ֖ים רַבִּ֑ים וַ⁠עֲשִׁירִ֖ים בַּ⁠שֵּׁ֥פֶל יֵשֵֽׁבוּ

**"into many high places, and rich {people} sit in the lowly place"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if important positions were **high places** and unimportant positions were **the lowly place**. It means that foolish people are given positions of honor and authority, while rich people are given positions of little importance. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[into many positions of honor, and rich people sit in positions of little importance]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_zqw7)

רָאִ֥יתִי עֲבָדִ֖ים עַל־סוּסִ֑ים וְ⁠שָׂרִ֛ים הֹלְכִ֥ים כַּ⁠עֲבָדִ֖ים עַל־הָ⁠אָֽרֶץ

**"Indeed, I saw slaves on horses and princes walking like slaves on the ground"**

Verses 6 and 7 mean basically the same thing. Verse 6 states the general idea, and verse 7 gives a specific example. Translate these phrases according to the decisions your team has made regarding translating parallelism in Hebrew poetry. Alternate translation: \[Indeed, I saw the social order reversed, with slaves holding high positions and princes brought low]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:7
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_o6oj)

עֲבָדִ֖ים עַל־סוּסִ֑ים

**"slaves on horses"**

The implication is that only important and wealthy people rode **horses**, while slaves walked. Qoheleth is saying that the normal order of society has been reversed. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[slaves riding on horses as if they were important and wealthy people]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:7
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ad9t)

חֹפֵ֥ר גּוּמָּ֖ץ בּ֣⁠וֹ יִפּ֑וֹל וּ⁠פֹרֵ֥ץ גָּדֵ֖ר יִשְּׁכֶ֥⁠נּוּ נָחָֽשׁ

**"One who digs a pit will fall into it, and one who breaks through a wall—a snake will bite him"**

Here Qoheleth uses a proverb in order to warn that people who do risky actions often suffer the consequences of those actions. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. Alternate translation: \[Whoever digs a hole may fall into it, and whoever tears down a wall may be bitten by a snake]

See: Proverbs

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:8
* **Associated Articles:** Proverbs (ID: `33425@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_hzab)

יִשְּׁכֶ֥⁠נּוּ נָחָֽשׁ

**"a snake will bite him"**

The implication is that snakes often lived in the cracks of stone walls, so a person who **breaks through a wall** could be bitten by a snake hiding in it. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[a snake hiding in the cracks of that wall will bite him]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:8
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_zzjm)

מַסִּ֣יעַ אֲבָנִ֔ים יֵעָצֵ֖ב בָּ⁠הֶ֑ם בּוֹקֵ֥עַ עֵצִ֖ים יִסָּ֥כֶן בָּֽ⁠ם

**"Indeed, one who pulls up stones will be hurt by them; one who splits wood will be endangered by them"**

Here Qoheleth uses or invents a proverb in order to teach that every kind of work carries its own risks, so a wise person takes care while doing it. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. This verse emphasizes the meaning of verse 8 by repeating the same idea using different words. Alternate translation: \[Whoever moves stones may be injured by them; whoever chops logs may be harmed by them]

See: Proverbs

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:9
* **Associated Articles:** Proverbs (ID: `33425@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_tun6)

מַסִּ֣יעַ אֲבָנִ֔ים יֵעָצֵ֖ב בָּ⁠הֶ֑ם בּוֹקֵ֥עַ עֵצִ֖ים יִסָּ֥כֶן בָּֽ⁠ם

**"Indeed, one who pulls up stones will be hurt by them; one who splits wood will be endangered by them"**

Verses 8 and 9 mean basically the same thing. Verse 9 emphasizes the meaning of verse 8 by repeating the same idea using different words. Translate these phrases according to the decisions your team has made regarding translating parallelism in Hebrew poetry. Alternate translation: \[Indeed, one who pulls up stones or splits wood will be hurt by them]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:9
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_jv2s)

יֵעָצֵ֖ב בָּ⁠הֶ֑ם

**"will be hurt by them"**

The expression **will be hurt** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. The word **them** refers to the stones. Alternate translation: \[will find that they hurt him]

* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

יִסָּ֥כֶן בָּֽ⁠ם

**"will be endangered by them"**

The expression **will be endangered** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. The word **them** refers to the split pieces of wood. Alternate translation: \[will find that the pieces endanger him]

* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_zzzj)

וְ⁠הוּא֙ לֹא־פָנִ֣ים קִלְקַ֔ל

**"but he himself does not lighten the faces"**

Qoheleth is using **lighten the faces** as a common expression of the culture to mean “sharpen the cutting edge.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. The pronoun **he** refers to the person who is using the axe to cut wood. Alternate translation: \[but the person using the axe does not sharpen its cutting edge]

וְ⁠יִתְר֥וֹן הכשיר חָכְמָֽה

**"But a profit to succeeding {is} wisdom"**

לְ⁠בַ֖עַל הַ⁠לָּשֽׁוֹן

**"for the master of the tongue"**

Qoheleth is using **the master of the tongue** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the one who charms snakes with his words.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Ecclesiastes 10:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_jifu)

דִּבְרֵ֥י פִי־חָכָ֖ם חֵ֑ן וְ⁠שִׂפְת֥וֹת כְּסִ֖יל תְּבַלְּעֶֽ⁠נּוּ

**"The} words of {the} mouth of a wise {person are} favor, but {the} lips of a fool will swallow him"**

Here Qoheleth uses a proverb in order to teach that wise people speak in ways that bring them goodwill, while fools destroy themselves by what they say. Translate this proverb in a way that will be recognized as a proverb and be meaningful in your language and culture. Alternate translation: \[What a wise person says brings him goodwill, but what a fool says will destroy him]

See: Proverbs

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:12
* **Associated Articles:** Proverbs (ID: `33425@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_q9gj)

חֵ֑ן

**"are} favor"**

If your language does not use a noun for the idea of **favor**, you could express the same idea in another way. This could mean: (1\) the words of a wise person are gracious and pleasing. Alternate translation: \[are gracious and pleasing]; or (2\) the words of a wise person win him the goodwill of others. Alternate translation: \[brings him goodwill]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:12
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_scbz)

תְּחִלַּ֥ת דִּבְרֵי־פִ֖י⁠הוּ סִכְל֑וּת וְ⁠אַחֲרִ֣ית פִּ֔י⁠הוּ הוֹלֵל֖וּת רָעָֽה

**"The beginning of the words of his mouth {is} foolishness, and {the} end of his mouth {is} evil insanity"**

Qoheleth is referring to the whole of a fool’s speech by naming two extremes, **The beginning of the words of his mouth** and **the end of his mouth**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[At first the words of his mouth are foolishness, and in the end what he says is evil insanity]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:13
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_ja28)

הוֹלֵל֖וּת רָעָֽה

**"is} evil insanity"**

If your language does not use a noun for the idea of **insanity**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[is wickedly insane]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:13
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_rtes)

הָ⁠אָדָם֙

**"Humanity"**

Qoheleth is not referring to a specific person when he says **Humanity** and **him**. He means people in general, not the fool mentioned earlier in the verse. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[A person]

See: Generic Noun Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:14
* **Associated Articles:** Generic Noun Phrases (ID: `33277@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_ftik)

וַ⁠אֲשֶׁ֤ר יִֽהְיֶה֙ מֵֽ⁠אַחֲרָ֔י⁠ו

**"and that which will be after him"**

Qoheleth is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and does not know what will happen after he is gone]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:14
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_f8dm)

מִ֖י יַגִּ֥יד

**"who will declare {it"**

Qoheleth is using the question form for emphasis. He means that no one can tell a person what will happen after him. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[no one at all can declare it]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:14
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_dwji)

תְּיַגְּעֶ֑⁠נּוּ

**"wearies him"**

The pronoun **him** refers to each one of the **foolish** people mentioned earlier in the verse. Qoheleth changes from the plural to the singular here. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[wearies each one of them]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:15
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_dsbe)

לֹֽא־יָדַ֖ע לָ⁠לֶ֥כֶת אֶל־עִֽיר

**"he does not know {how} to go to a city"**

Qoheleth is using the expression **he does not know how to go to a city** as a common expression of the culture to mean “he is so foolish that he cannot even find his way to town.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[he is too foolish to find even the most obvious path]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stupid (ID: `keyterm:Stupid`)

## Ecclesiastes 10:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_p4z5)

אִֽי־לָ֣⁠ךְ

**"Woe to you"**

Qoheleth is using a phrase that expresses a strong feeling of grief about the trouble that will come to a land with such leaders. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[How terrible it will be for you]

## Ecclesiastes 10:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_ohhb)

אֶ֔רֶץ

**"land"**

Qoheleth is speaking to a **land** that he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about the harm that bad rulers cause to a nation. He is using **land** to mean the people who live in it. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about the land. Alternate translation: \[people of the land]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:16
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 10:16 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_ys62)

נָ֑עַר

**"is} a youth"**

The implication is that a king who is **a youth** is not able to rule well. This could mean: (1\) the king is young and inexperienced. Alternate translation: \[is too young and inexperienced to rule well]; or (2\) the king is a person of low birth, such as a servant, since the word translated **youth** can also mean “servant.” Alternate translation: \[is not able to rule well]. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

**"and your princes eat in the morning"**

The implication is that **your princes** are feasting **in the morning**, when they should be working to govern the land. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[and your princes feast in the morning when they should be governing you]

## Ecclesiastes 10:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_ywzn)

אַשְׁרֵ֣י⁠ךְ אֶ֔רֶץ שֶׁ⁠מַּלְכֵּ֖⁠ךְ בֶּן־חוֹרִ֑ים וְ⁠שָׂרַ֨יִ⁠ךְ֙ בָּ⁠עֵ֣ת יֹאכֵ֔לוּ בִּ⁠גְבוּרָ֖ה וְ⁠לֹ֥א בַ⁠שְּׁתִֽי

**"Blessed are you, land, that your king {is} a son of nobles and {that} your princes will eat at the time with might and not with drunkenness"**

Verses 16 and 17 mean basically the same thing. For emphasis, Qoheleth is saying it first negatively and then positively. If a speaker of your language would not use repetition like this, in your translation you can convey the emphasis in a different way. Alternate translation: \[Blessed are you, land, that your king is a son of nobles and that your princes will eat at the proper time only to gain strength]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 10:17
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 11 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_w7be)

Ecclesiastes 11 Chapter Introduction
====================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter urges readers to act diligently and generously even though they cannot know what the future holds, and then to enjoy life, especially in youth, while remembering that God will judge them.

1. Acting wisely despite an uncertain future (1–6\)

    1. Give generously, since no one knows what misfortune may come (1–2\)
        2. Do not wait for perfect conditions before working (3–4\)
        3. No one knows what God is doing, so keep working (5–6\)
2. Enjoy life while remembering the days of darkness (7–8\)
3. Counsel to the young man: enjoy your youth, but know that God will judge you (9–10\)

The counsel to the young man in verses 9–10 continues into 12:1–8, so translators may want to keep the end of this chapter connected with the beginning of the next.

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 11:1–4 and 11:6–8\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Vapor

The Hebrew word rendered “vapor” is this book’s key term for what is fleeting and insubstantial, and it appears again in this chapter in verses 8 and 10\. Render it consistently through the book.

### God’s judgment

In verse 9, Qoheleth tells the young man to enjoy life but to know that God will judge him for all that he does. The call to enjoy life is therefore not permission to live carelessly.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### What people cannot know

The phrase “you do not know” or “you are not knowing” recurs in verses 2, 5, and 6, and it ties the chapter’s advice together: because people cannot know the future or the work of God, they should act wisely and diligently now. If possible, translate these phrases in a similar way so that readers can see the connection.

### Proverbs with uncertain meaning

The sayings in verses 1–2 are brief and figurative, and scholars understand them in different ways, such as being generous to others or spreading out one’s trade or investments. Translate them in a way that allows for these meanings if possible. (See: Proverbs)

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 11:0
* **Associated Articles:** Proverbs (ID: `33425@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 11:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_xc90)

שַׁלַּ֥ח לַחְמְ⁠ךָ֖ עַל־פְּנֵ֣י הַ⁠מָּ֑יִם כִּֽי־בְ⁠רֹ֥ב הַ⁠יָּמִ֖ים תִּמְצָאֶֽ⁠נּוּ

**"Throw your bread on the face of the waters, for in many days you will find it"**

Here Qoheleth is speaking as if a person’s goods or efforts were **bread** thrown **on the face of the waters**, by which he means that they are given away in some sense. This could refer to: (1\) being generous to others, which will bring a reward later, as in the UST, or (2\) being willing to risk making an investment in something that should bring a profit at a future time. Alternate translation: \[make investments now, because they will bring profit to you later]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 11:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 11:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_jr4w)

**"to seven and even to eight"**

Qoheleth is using **to seven and even to eight** as a common expression of the culture to mean “to many.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[to many people]

See: Idiom

## Ecclesiastes 11:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_b2b7)

## Ecclesiastes - Chapter 12 Introduction (id: en_tn_ECC_qlgp)

Ecclesiastes 12 Chapter Introduction
====================================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter completes Qoheleth’s counsel to the young person from 11:9–10, repeats the book’s opening refrain, and then closes the book with words about Qoheleth and a final command to fear God.

1. Remember your Creator before old age and death come (1–7\)

    1. Before the days of misfortune come (1–2\)
        2. Pictures of the weakness of old age (3–5\)
        3. Pictures of death (6–7\)
2. The final refrain: everything is vapor (8\)
3. Words about Qoheleth and the sayings of the wise (9–12\)

    1. Qoheleth’s work as a teacher (9–10\)
        2. The value and limits of wise sayings (11–12\)
4. The conclusion: fear God and keep his commandments (13–14\)

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetry in 12:1–7 and 12:13–14\.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Vapor

The refrain in verse 8 repeats the words of 1:2, so that the book’s key term for what is fleeting and insubstantial frames everything Qoheleth has said. Render it the same way you did in chapter 1\.

### Death as a return

In verse 7, the body that God formed from dust returns to the earth, and the spirit that God gave returns to him. Verse 5 similarly calls the grave a person’s “eternal home.”

### Fearing God and God’s judgment

The book ends by telling people to fear God and keep his commandments, because God will bring every deed into judgment. This is reverent submission to God, not terror.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Pictures of old age

Verses 2–6 describe old age and death through a long series of images, such as a house whose guards tremble, grinders that stop working, and a lamp and a well that break. Many interpreters understand these as pictures of a weakening body, while others take them as a literal household and town in decline. If possible, keep the images so that readers can see this extended picture.

### Qoheleth spoken about in the third person

From verse 8 to the end of the book, someone speaks about Qoheleth instead of Qoheleth speaking for himself. Make sure that it is clear in your translation that a different speaker is now describing Qoheleth and his teaching.

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:0


## Ecclesiastes 12:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_pzlh)

וּ⁠זְכֹר֙ אֶת־בּ֣וֹרְאֶ֔י⁠ךָ

**"So, remember your Creator"**

Qoheleth is using **remember** as a common expression of the culture to mean “keep in mind and honor.” He is not talking about recalling something that a person has forgotten. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[So, honor your Creator and keep him in mind]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)


## Ecclesiastes 12:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_gfgu)

עַ֣ד אֲשֶׁ֤ר לֹא־יָבֹ֨אוּ֙ יְמֵ֣י הָֽ⁠רָעָ֔ה וְ⁠הִגִּ֣יעוּ שָׁנִ֔ים

**"when the days of misfortune have not come and the years arrive"**

Qoheleth is using the phrase **when the days of misfortune have not come** to mean “before the days of misfortune come.” The negation also applies to **the years arrive**, so the meaning is “before the years arrive.” Your language may have a connecting word or phrase that you can use to show that the young person should remember his Creator before this time comes. Alternate translation: \[before the days of misfortune come and before the years arrive]

See: Connect — Sequential Time Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:1
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Sequential Time Relationship (ID: `33333@UWTranslationManual`)

The implication is that **the days of misfortune** are the time of old age, when a person experiences weakness, pain, and other troubles. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the days of old age when you will have weakness and pain]

## Ecclesiastes 12:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_r8be)

לֹֽא־תֶחְשַׁ֤ךְ הַ⁠שֶּׁ֨מֶשׁ֙ וְ⁠הָ⁠א֔וֹר וְ⁠הַ⁠יָּרֵ֖חַ וְ⁠הַ⁠כּוֹכָבִ֑ים

**"the sun and the light and the moon and the stars have not darkened"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if what happens to a person in old age were the sun, the moon, and the stars becoming **darkened**. This could mean: (1\) that the joy and strength of a person’s life fades in old age, just as the lights in the sky grow dark. Alternate translation: \[your joy and strength have not faded like the light of the sun, the moon, and the stars growing dark]; or (2\) that an old person’s eyesight grows dim, so that even the lights in the sky seem dark. Alternate translation: \[your eyes have not grown so dim that the sun, the moon, and the stars seem dark to you]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

Qoheleth is speaking as if the troubles of old age were **the clouds** that **return after the rain**. He means that in old age one trouble follows another without relief, just as storm clouds come back again even after it has already rained. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression, or you could state the meaning plainly. Qoheleth is speaking as if a person’s life were a lamp, a **bowl of gold** that hangs on a **cord of silver**. **"is removed"**

The phrase **is removed** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text as we have it today. There is however a correction, “is snapped,” in the margin of the text that Hebrew scribes have recommended. This correction fits well with the image of a cord that breaks and lets a lamp fall. You may want to follow this correction in your translation, or you may want to follow the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[is snapped]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Alternative Readings (ID: `en_ta_translate_translate-alternativereadings@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Pit (ID: `realia:Pit`); Clay Jar (ID: `realia:ClayJar`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ECC_gdit)

וְ⁠תִשָּׁ֤בֶר כַּד֙ עַל־הַ⁠מַּבּ֔וּעַ וְ⁠נָרֹ֥ץ הַ⁠גַּלְגַּ֖ל אֶל־הַ⁠בּֽוֹר

**"and the pitcher is shattered at the fountain and the wheel is crushed at the cistern"**

Qoheleth is speaking as if a person’s life were **the pitcher** that people use to draw water **at the fountain** and **the wheel** that people use to lift water **at the cistern**. He means that just as these things can no longer bring up water once they are broken, a person’s life ends when he dies. If this meaning would not be clear in your language, you could use a comparable expression or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and a person’s life comes to an end the way a broken pitcher and a crushed wheel can no longer draw up water]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Pit (ID: `realia:Pit`); Clay Jar (ID: `realia:ClayJar`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ECC_nbpv)

וְ⁠תִשָּׁ֤בֶר כַּד֙ עַל־הַ⁠מַּבּ֔וּעַ וְ⁠נָרֹ֥ץ הַ⁠גַּלְגַּ֖ל אֶל־הַ⁠בּֽוֹר

**"and the pitcher is shattered at the fountain and the wheel is crushed at the cistern"**

The expressions **is shattered** and **is crushed** are passive verbal forms. If it would be unnatural to use those forms, you could express the ideas in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and the pitcher shatters at the fountain, and the wheel breaks apart at the cistern]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_lqd4)

וְ⁠יָשֹׁ֧ב הֶ⁠עָפָ֛ר עַל־הָ⁠אָ֖רֶץ כְּ⁠שֶׁ⁠הָיָ֑ה

**"and the dust returns to the earth as that it was"**

Here, **the dust** represents the body which God formed from dust. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and the body returns to the earth as the dust that it was]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_g5n3)

וְ⁠יֹתֵ֕ר שֶׁ⁠הָיָ֥ה קֹהֶ֖לֶת חָכָ֑ם ע֗וֹד לִמַּד

**"More {than} that Qoheleth was wise, again he taught"**

The phrase **More than that** means “in addition to the fact that.” The author is saying that besides the fact that **Qoheleth was wise**, he also **taught the people knowledge**. If the connection between this statement and the previous one is not clear, you may want to use a different connecting word to show how this statement relates to what comes before it. Alternate translation: \[In addition to being wise, Qoheleth also taught]

See: Connecting Words and Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:9
* **Associated Articles:** Connecting Words and Phrases (ID: `33335@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Proverb (ID: `keyterm:Saying`); Intention (ID: `keyterm:Intention`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_xkr8)

וְ⁠אִזֵּ֣ן וְ⁠חִקֵּ֔ר תִּקֵּ֖ן מְשָׁלִ֥ים הַרְבֵּֽה

**"and he weighed and he searched. He ordered many proverbs"**

The author is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Here, **weighed** means “carefully evaluated.” Alternate translation: \[and he carefully evaluated proverbs, and he searched them out and set them in order]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:9
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Proverb (ID: `keyterm:Saying`); Intention (ID: `keyterm:Intention`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_z29n)

דִּבְרֵי־חֵ֑פֶץ

**"words of pleasure"**

The author is using the possessive form to describe **words** that are characterized by **pleasure**, that is, words that are pleasing or fitting. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[pleasing words]

## Ecclesiastes 12:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_im3x)

דִּבְרֵ֤י חֲכָמִים֙ כַּ⁠דָּ֣רְבֹנ֔וֹת

**"The} words of wise {people are} like goads"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **goads** are pointed sticks that prod cattle to move in the right direction, so the **words of wise people** prod people to think and live in the right way. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[The words of wise people prod people to think and live in the right way, as goads prod cattle to move in the right direction]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:11
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Goad (ID: `realia:Goad`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Nail (ID: `realia:Nail`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_fcle)

וּֽ⁠כְ⁠מַשְׂמְר֥וֹת נְטוּעִ֖ים

**"and like nails implanted"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **nails** that someone has driven firmly into wood stay fixed in place, so the collected sayings of wise people are firm and lasting. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and as firm and lasting as nails driven deeply into wood]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:11
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

בַּעֲלֵ֣י אֲסֻפּ֑וֹת

**"are} masters of collections"**

The author is using **masters of collections** as a common expression of the culture. This could mean: (1\) that the expression refers to the collected sayings of wise people. Alternate translation: \[are the collected sayings of wise people]; or (2\) that the expression refers to the people who collect wise sayings. ## Ecclesiastes 12:11 (#6) (id: en_tn_ECC_o6km)

מֵ⁠רֹעֶ֥ה אֶחָֽד

**"one Shepherd"**

The author is speaking as if the one who gives wise sayings were a **Shepherd** who guides his sheep. This could mean: (1\) that **one Shepherd** refers to God, who is the source of all wisdom and who guides his people as a shepherd guides sheep. Alternate translation: \[God, who guides his people like a shepherd]; or (2\) that the expression refers to a wise teacher or king who guides people with wise sayings. Alternate translation: \[one wise teacher, who guides people like a shepherd]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Ecclesiastes 12:11
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Ecclesiastes 12:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ECC_rpsh)

בְּנִ֣⁠י

**"my son"**

Here the masculine term **son** has a generic sense that includes both men and women. The author is using it to address anyone who learns from him. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use language in your translation that is clearly inclusive of both men and women. Alternate translation: \[my student]

See: When Masculine Words Include Women

## Ecclesiastes 12:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_ECC_f8av)

זֶ֖ה כָּל־הָ⁠אָדָֽם

**"this is the whole of humanity"**

The author is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. This could mean: (1\) that the author means that fearing God and keeping his commandments is the whole duty of every person. Alternate translation: \[this is the whole duty of every person]; or (2\) that the author means that this is what every person was created for. Alternate translation: \[this is what every person was made for]; or (3\) that the author means that this applies to every person. Alternate translation: \[this applies to every person]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)
